27:["$","table",null,{"children":[["$","thead",null,{"children":["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","th",null,{"children":"Reuse"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"What is saved"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"What still runs"}]]}]}],["$","tbody",null,{"children":[["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Shared state within one request"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"The model’s processing of the common context"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Each independent question and its answer"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Prefix cache across requests"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Compatible attention keys and values from an earlier call"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"The uncached suffix, new attention, and answer computation"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Application result cache"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"A validated answer or application result"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Cache lookup, freshness checks, and applying the result"}]]}]]}]]}]
28:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Within one request: process the state once"}]
29:["$","p",null,{"children":["Put the passage in ",["$","code",null,{"children":"state"}]," and the three decisions in ",["$","code",null,{"children":"questions"}],". TypeSafe documents that Jev ingests the state once and evaluates the questions in parallel. Repeating the passage inside every question adds redundant input. Batching helps when the evidence is shared and the decisions are independent; a verification question that needs the selected name still belongs in a later call. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://docs.typesafe.ai/models","children":"Model behavior"}],", ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://docs.typesafe.ai/patterns/fan-out","children":"Fan-out pattern"}],"."]}]
2a:["$","p",null,{"children":["To understand the neural-network mechanism, start with the ",["$","a",null,{"href":"/writing/transformer-basics-prefill-and-decode/#prefill-reads-the-prompt-decode-extends-it","children":"prefill and decode article"}],". In a causal Transformer, prefill runs the known context through the layers. Each attention layer saves ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"keys and values, K/V, for the processed token positions"}],". These are numeric activations computed from this particular input. Keeping the trained weights loaded is separate from saving those input-specific calculations."]}]
2b:["$","p",null,{"children":["A new question still passes through the Transformer. At each layer, its queries read the shared context’s saved K/V plus its own permitted positions. Attention, feed-forward transformations, and the answer readout still run for that question. Independent branches can share the prefix while keeping their question positions separate. Causal attention makes this possible: earlier positions cannot depend on later questions, so those questions cannot change the cached prefix. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/main/cache_explanation","children":"KV-cache mechanics"}],"."]}]
2c:["$","p",null,{"children":["This describes a concrete way to implement shared work. Harsha Gundala’s ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://huggingface.co/harshatheg/Qwen-2.5-1B-RLCD/blob/main/core/engine_torch.py","children":"public inference engine"}],", discussed in the ",["$","a",null,{"href":"/writing/jev-and-system-one-models/#what-shared-prefill-means","children":"earlier Jev article"}],", batches question suffixes over a shared prefix using an existing Qwen model. Jev’s exact internal layout is unpublished. The API’s three questions do not establish three separate neural heads."]}]
2d:["$","$Ldf",null,{}]
2e:["$","$Le0",null,{"summary":"The tensor shapes, and why later attention still costs work","children":[["$","p",null,{"children":["For a conventional attention layer, let ",["$","code",null,{"children":"X"}]," contain the current token representations. Learned projections produce ",["$","code",null,{"children":"Q = X W_Q"}],", ",["$","code",null,{"children":"K = X W_K"}],", and ",["$","code",null,{"children":"V = X W_V"}],", with position handling applied as required by the architecture. A typical per-layer cache has this shape:"]}],["$","figure",null,{"data-rehype-pretty-code-figure":"","children":["$","pre",null,{"tabIndex":"0","data-language":"text","data-theme":"one-light one-dark-pro","children":["$","code",null,{"data-language":"text","data-theme":"one-light one-dark-pro","style":{"display":"grid"},"children":[["$","span",null,{"data-line":"","children":["$","span",null,{"children":"cache[layer].K: [batch, kv_heads, prefix_tokens, head_dim]"}]}],"\n",["$","span",null,{"data-line":"","children":["$","span",null,{"children":"cache[layer].V: [batch, kv_heads, prefix_tokens, head_dim]"}]}],"\n",["$","span",null,{"data-line":"","children":" "}],"\n",["$","span",null,{"data-line":"","children":["$","span",null,{"children":"question attention:"}]}],"\n",["$","span",null,{"data-line":"","children":["$","span",null,{"children":"  Q_new attends to [K_prefix, K_new]"}]}],"\n",["$","span",null,{"data-line":"","children":["$","span",null,{"children":"  attention weights combine [V_prefix, V_new]"}]}]]}]}]}],["$","p",null,{"children":["The attention mask limits each question to the shared prefix and its own allowed positions. New queries still compare against the retained keys and mix their values, so long context continues to cost memory and attention work. The cache avoids recomputing the prefix through the layers. It is a collection of per-layer tensors, rather than one embedding for the paragraph. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://github.com/meta-llama/llama/blob/main/llama/model.py","children":"Llama’s projections, cache, and attention code"}],"."]}],["$","p",null,{"children":["Reusing state this way requires state representations that do not depend on the changing question. With full bidirectional attention over the concatenated state and question, changing a question can also change the state’s hidden representations. That architecture needs a different reuse scheme. See the ",["$","a",null,{"href":"/writing/transformer-basics-prefill-and-decode/#what-happens-inside-a-layer","children":"attention-mask explanation"}],"."]}]]}]
2f:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Across requests: a retained prefix or a saved answer"}]
30:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Provider prefix caching"}]," keeps compatible K/V available after a call. If the next request starts with the same token sequence under the same model and attention setup, the server can reuse that prefix. Asking a new question after the same passage can save the passage’s prefill. It still requires evaluating the question; a text-generating model also keeps its decoding loop. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/features/automatic_prefix_caching/","children":"vLLM’s prefix caching"}],"."]}]
31:["$","p",null,{"children":["The match is positional and exact. Edit an early token and subsequent cached blocks no longer have the same preceding context, even when their own text is unchanged. An unchanged rubric placed after changing state is therefore not automatically reusable as a prefix. Provider serialization, cache capacity, eviction, and routing determine which work is actually reused. Reordering JSON fields is not a supported way to control Jev’s internal token layout. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/design/prefix_caching/","children":"How vLLM identifies cached blocks"}],"."]}]
32:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"For Jev, rely on the documented sharing within a call."}]," As of September 25, 2026, the public native API reference does not specify a cache lifetime, a cross-request cache control, or a cached-token usage field. Vercel’s general automatic prompt-caching documentation also does not establish that feature for Jev evaluation calls. I would budget separate calls without an assumed cache discount until the provider documents one. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://docs.typesafe.ai/api","children":"TypeSafe API"}],", ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://vercel.com/docs/ai-gateway/models-and-providers/automatic-caching","children":"Vercel caching support"}],"."]}]
33:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Application result caching"}]," is under your control. The interactive demos in this article keep a successfully validated result for one hour in server memory. An identical provider, example, and option can return that result without calling Jev again. Simultaneous identical requests also share one in-progress operation. The UI labels reused results as cached. This cache belongs to one running server instance and disappears when that instance restarts; it contains results, not neural-network tensors."]}]
34:["$","p",null,{"children":"The demos can use a short cache key because their inputs and question definitions are fixed by the code. A general application should include the provider/model version, state, questions and criteria, relevant settings, and data/schema versions in the key, with an appropriate user or tenant scope. Changing any evidence needed for the answer must invalidate it. Cache validated successes; handle provider failures separately. An expiry time limits reuse but cannot make changed evidence fresh."}]
35:["$","p",null,{"children":"Finally, cache preprocessing where it helps: the transcript for an unchanged video, embeddings for unchanged passages, or a parsed page snapshot. A new search query can reuse those artifacts while still needing fresh relevance judgments. Keep each artifact tied to its source and preprocessing version, especially when saved offsets will be applied back to text."}]

40:["$","span",null,{"id":"gliner-jev-extraction-before-judgment"}]
41:["$","h2",null,{"children":"Example 2: GLiNER + Jev"}]
42:["$","$Le1",null,{"project":"gliner"}]
43:["$","p",null,{"children":"Suppose you want a graph built from news articles that you can ask questions of: which companies has Northwind invested in, and which of its deals are still pending? Building it takes two different skills."}]
44:["$","p",null,{"children":["The first is spotting candidate facts. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://github.com/urchade/GLiNER","children":"GLiNER"}]," is a small extraction model: give it text and a list of labels, and it marks the matching spans, such as companies and places. Later versions such as ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://github.com/fastino-ai/GLiNER2","children":"GLiNER2"}]," also propose relations between those spans. It is cheap enough to run over every article. It matches the shape of a sentence, though, so “Reports said Northwind would sell its Canadian brokerage; the company denied them” still yields ",["$","code",null,{"children":"Northwind SELLS Canadian brokerage"}],"."]}]
45:["$","p",null,{"children":["The second is reading how the article presents each candidate: as done, as possible, as denied, or as planned. That is a judgment with a small fixed set of answers, which is what Jev is for. ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://lyonwj.com/blog/typesafe-jev-knowledge-graph-extraction","children":"William Lyon’s knowledge-graph experiments"}]," pair the two. GLiNER2.5 proposes the edges, and Jev asks two questions of each one: does the article discuss this relationship at all, and how does it present it?"]}]
46:["$","$L104",null,{}]
47:["$","p",null,{"children":["The judgment is stored on the edge, so nothing is thrown away. A facts query filters on ",["$","code",null,{"children":"assertion = 'asserted'"}],"; a question about pending deals asks for ",["$","code",null,{"children":"hypothetical"}]," or ",["$","code",null,{"children":"forward_looking"}],". In Lyon’s test the gate raised precision from 0.279 to 0.404 without losing a single correct fact. The 56 edges it set aside were ones the documents never connected, or connected and then hedged or denied, and GLiNER’s own confidence scores could not tell them apart."]}]

6b:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","a",null,{"href":"https://github.com/browser-use/jev-ultrafast","children":"Browser Use’s Jev experiment"}]," drives a real browser one step at a time: observe the page, make one Jev request, validate the answer, act, and observe again. Its ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://github.com/browser-use/jev-ultrafast/blob/main/docs/performance.md","children":"performance report"}]," also shows how much the surrounding runtime matters. In three paired runs, an optimized runtime cut median browser protocol calls from 1,092 to 101 and Jev requests from 22 to 17, and median task time fell 25%. The creator notes that three pairs are too few for a strong statistical claim."]}]

6d:["$","p",null,{"children":["Each step starts inside the page. A snapshot script lists the visible controls with their accessible name, role, current value, and on-screen rectangle. The executor keeps the rectangles and its own action IDs (",["$","code",null,{"children":"e1"}],", ",["$","code",null,{"children":"e2"}],", …). Jev receives a numbered list of elements and answers four Choices in one request: which operation to run, and, for each operation, which element it would act on. Only the target answer matching the chosen operation is used. Asking every target question up front costs a few extra answers and saves a second round trip once the operation is known."]}]

7a:["$","p",null,{"children":["It asks all five in one request, including questions whose relevance depends on answers it does not have yet. 80:["$","h2",null,{"children":"Example 7: Clip Fast"}]
81:["$","$Le1",null,{"project":"clipping"}]
82:["$","p",null,{"children":"Clipping is semantic search over a video. Ask for the moments where a speaker explains a topic, and get back source intervals an editor can preview and cut. A topic can come up several times in one talk, so the search needs to find every match."}]
83:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","a",null,{"href":"https://x.com/BurhanUsman/status/2101641842441732297","children":"Burhan’s clipping demo on X"}]," is a good example. He reports topic-based selection from a recording longer than 90 minutes in under two seconds for roughly two cents, and links his ",["$","a",null,{"href":"https://clipfast.lol/","children":"Clip Fast beta"}]," in the thread. Those are the creator’s figures; the post does not say whether transcription or export is included. Clip Fast’s code is not public, so the design below is mine."]}]
84:["$","$Le2",null,{"project":"clipping"}]
85:["$","p",null,{"children":"It follows Needle’s pattern. A transcriber turns each video into sentences with start and end times. Code cuts each transcript into short windows of a few sentences, and each window gets its own yes/no question: does this passage explain the topic? All the windows of one transcript share a request, with the whole transcript as context, and every video’s request goes out at the same time. Because each window is judged independently, one video can match in several places."}]
86:["$","$L1b9",null,{}]
87:["$","p",null,{"children":"Neighboring windows that matched become one candidate clip; an unmatched window in between starts a new one. Window edges are arbitrary, so a second request trims every candidate to whole sentences, choosing where each clip should start and end. Code reads those sentences’ times from the transcript; no model writes a timestamp. A third request checks that each clip makes sense to someone who has not seen the rest of the video."}]
88:["$","p",null,{"children":"That is three rounds of requests however many clips there are. A 90-minute talk cut into 30-second windows is about 240 yes/no questions, spread over a few parallel requests of about 20 minutes of transcript each, then one request to trim and one to check."}]

a1:["$","p",null,{"children":"When you can write the pattern, write it: a Cypher query is exact, fast, and needs no model.
